My sister says I should just let it go, but that’s easy to say when you’re not the one being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these events are somehow related to you or your life in a special way, beyond just being targeted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “special,” exactly… but it is related to me, because I’m the one who notices. I’m the one asking questions and keeping track. For months it’s been the same kind of thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back—and everyone acts like these are separate little mistakes.\n\nBut they’re not separate. When you’ve worked in offices like I have, you know how things are supposed to go. I used to supervise people doing this kind of work, and you learn very quickly when something is just sloppy and when information is being held back. And somehow it always ends with me being pushed out of decisions I should be part of. So yes, it feels connected to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people who are supposedly doing these things to you are plotting against you in a coordinated way?\n\nPatient: Coordinated… I don’t know if they’re all sitting in a room making a plan, if that’s what you mean. But it feels connected. For months now it’s the same pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved, then the bills are wrong, then someone “forgets” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the one left without the information.\n\nI worked in offices a long time, you know, and when you supervise people you learn how things are supposed to flow—messages, records, callbacks, all of that. My old manager used to say, “One mistake is a mistake, three mistakes is a system,” and that’s sort of what I keep thinking about. Everyone tells me it’s just errors, but I know when information is being kept from me. It’s like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I’m supposed to be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these events are happening because of something specific about you, such as your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s my thoughts, like I’m making it happen by thinking something. But my actions, maybe—because I keep asking questions. I keep noticing when things don’t add up.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, really. For months it’s been the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, bills come wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone says, oh, it’s just mistakes, but I worked in offices, I know how records and calls are supposed to be handled. If you keep pressing, some people don’t like that.\n\nIt reminds me of when I was supervising staff and someone would “misplace” paperwork after being corrected—anyway, that’s what I mean. It feels like because I’m the one keeping track, they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there could be another explanation for these events, other than someone intentionally trying to keep information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything is possible, sure. People do make mistakes. I’ve made mistakes in offices myself—once we had a whole stack of invoices filed under the wrong vendor because someone changed the label and nobody told the rest of us, and then everyone blamed the front desk.\n\nBut this is why I’m here, because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be mistaken about someone intentionally keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking… could I be mistaken. I mean, in a general sense, yes, people can be wrong. I know that. I supervised office staff for years, and half the job was sorting out misunderstandings—someone puts a note in the wrong folder, someone assumes someone else made the call, that kind of thing.\n\nBut this doesn’t feel like that. I’m here because for months now I keep seeing the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, the bills show up wrong, people “forget” to call me back, and then I’m the one who doesn’t have the information. And everyone keeps saying it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs about people keeping information from you could be considered unreasonable by others?\n\nPatient: I’m sure they would call it unreasonable. My sister already gives me that look, like I’m making too much of it. But I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern—appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in offices, so I notice these things. There’s a way information flows when people want you included, and there’s a way it suddenly gets messy when they don’t. So maybe other people think I’m overreacting, but to me it’s pretty clear they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you could change your mind about people keeping information from you if you found out more information?\n\nPatient: I mean… if there were real information, yes, I would look at it. I’m not someone who refuses paperwork or facts—honestly, in my old office, I was usually the one telling people to check the forms before jumping to conclusions. We had this one woman who would swear the copier was “eating” her reports, and it turned out she was saving them to the wrong folder every time.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern with my sister—appointments moved around, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the last to know. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after a while it stops looking like mistakes. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions. So… I guess if I found out more, maybe, but it would have to explain all of that, not just one little piece.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your belief about people keeping information from you is something that you are completely certain about, or is it something that you could potentially change your mind about?\n\nPatient: Completely certain is a strong phrase… I mean, I suppose anyone could change their mind if someone actually showed them something that made sense. I’m not trying to be stubborn for the sake of it.\n\nBut I’ve been around offices long enough to know when things are just normal confusion and when they keep happening in the same direction. With my sister, it’s been months of this—appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m the one left out, every time.\n\nSo could I change my mind? Maybe, if there were a real explanation. But right now, no, I feel pretty certain. It really seems like information is being kept from me, like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day thinking about the possibility that people are keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it. I don’t sit down with a timer or anything, but it comes up a lot during the day—especially when I’m waiting for a call back or a bill doesn’t look right, or my sister tells me something after the fact.\n\nI’ll try to go do something else, and then I’ll think, well, why didn’t they tell me that sooner? At work, you know, if one person was always left off the email chain, that usually meant something. So I can’t just brush it off.\n\nI’d say… several hours altogether, probably. Not all at once, but on and off. Because for months now it’s been the same pattern—appointments moved, bills wrong, people “forgetting” to call—and everyone says mistakes, but it feels too consistent. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people keeping information from you are doing it for a reason that doesn't make sense to most people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it “doesn’t make sense” to most people. I think if they saw the whole thing laid out, maybe it would make more sense. Like, in an office, if one person keeps getting left off meeting notes, and then their paperwork is always late, and then people say, “Oh, sorry, I forgot”—after a while you stop calling that random.\n\nWith my sister, it’s been the same kind of thing for months. Her appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people don’t call me back, and then everyone acts like I’m overreacting. But I know they’re keeping information from me. I think they’re trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nNow, whether other people understand the reason… I don’t know. People don’t always understand office politics either until they’re the one being cut out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes, sometimes. I mean, I still get things done—I’m not sitting there unable to function—but it does pull my attention away.

Like at work, if someone makes a routine mistake, something I probably would’ve just corrected before, I find myself pushing harder now. Asking why it happened, who told them what, whether anyone else knew. And then the conversation gets uncomfortable, because people look at me like I’m making more of it than I should. But in an office, details matter. I’ve supervised people long enough to know that.

And with family meetings about my sister, it’s gotten tense. I’ll try to listen, but then I’m watching for what isn’t being said, or why I’m only hearing something at the last minute. So yes, it affects my focus some. I’m still managing my responsibilities, but people are definitely reacting differently to me now.

So maybe other people think I’m overreacting, but to me it’s pretty clear they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you could change your mind about people keeping information from you if you found out more information?\n\nPatient: I mean… if there were real information, yes, I would look at it. I’m not someone who refuses paperwork or facts—honestly, in my old office, I was usually the one telling people to check the forms before jumping to conclusions. We had this one woman who would swear the copier was “eating” her reports, and it turned out she was saving them to the wrong folder every time.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern with my sister—appointments moved around, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the last to know. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after a while it stops looking like mistakes. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions. So… I guess if I found out more, maybe, but it would have to explain all of that, not just one little piece.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your belief about people keeping information from you is something that you are completely certain about, or is it something that you could potentially change your mind about?\n\nPatient: Completely certain is a strong phrase… I mean, I suppose anyone could change their mind if someone actually showed them something that made sense. I’m not trying to be stubborn for the sake of it.\n\nBut I’ve been around offices long enough to know when things are just normal confusion and when they keep happening in the same direction. With my sister, it’s been months of this—appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m the one left out, every time.\n\nSo could I change my mind? Maybe, if there were a real explanation. But right now, no, I feel pretty certain. It really seems like information is being kept from me, like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day thinking about the possibility that people are keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it. I don’t sit down with a timer or anything, but it comes up a lot during the day—especially when I’m waiting for a call back or a bill doesn’t look right, or my sister tells me something after the fact.\n\nI’ll try to go do something else, and then I’ll think, well, why didn’t they tell me that sooner? At work, you know, if one person was always left off the email chain, that usually meant something. So I can’t just brush it off.\n\nI’d say… several hours altogether, probably. Not all at once, but on and off. Because for months now it’s been the same pattern—appointments moved, bills wrong, people “forgetting” to call—and everyone says mistakes, but it feels too consistent. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people keeping information from you are doing it for a reason that doesn't make sense to most people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it “doesn’t make sense” to most people. I think if they saw the whole thing laid out, maybe it would make more sense. Like, in an office, if one person keeps getting left off meeting notes, and then their paperwork is always late, and then people say, “Oh, sorry, I forgot”—after a while you stop calling that random.\n\nWith my sister, it’s been the same kind of thing for months. Her appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people don’t call me back, and then everyone acts like I’m overreacting. But I know they’re keeping information from me. I think they’re trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nNow, whether other people understand the reason… I don’t know. People don’t always understand office politics either until they’re the one being cut out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on other things because you're thinking about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. I mean, I still get things done—I’m not sitting there unable to function—but it does pull my attention away.\n\nLike at work, if someone makes a routine mistake, something I probably would’ve just corrected before, I find myself pushing harder now. Asking why it happened, who told them what, whether anyone else knew. And then the conversation gets uncomfortable, because people look at me like I’m making more of it than I should. But in an office, details matter. I’ve supervised people long enough to know that.\n\nAnd with family meetings about my sister, it’s gotten tense. I’ll try to listen, but then I’m watching for what isn’t being said, or why I’m only hearing something at the last minute. So yes, it affects my focus some. I’m still managing my responsibilities, but people are definitely reacting differently to me now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’m still managing my responsibilities, but it has started to affect how people respond to me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… yes, in a way. I still do things, I’m not just sitting around staring at the wall or anything. But it’s harder to enjoy them when part of my mind is going back over who said what, or why I heard something late.

And it spills over. Family meetings have become tense, because I’m listening for what’s being left out, and at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I used to. I used to be able to let some things go, you know, depending on the situation.

So I’m still managing my responsibilities. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But it has started to affect how people respond to me, and that makes it harder to relax and enjoy things.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because things have been happening for months now, and people keep telling me it’s just little mistakes, but it’s not. My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget”—and I know how offices work, I supervised that kind of thing for years, so I can tell when something is being handled normally and when it isn’t.\n\nIt’s like information is being kept from me. Everyone acts like I’m overreacting, but there’s a pattern. And I feel like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of. I mean, even the way messages get passed along—well, not passed along, that’s the point. It’s just… I came in because I need someone to understand that this isn’t just random.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally doing these things to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t think all of it is accidental. Not after this many months. My sister’s appointments get shifted, then a bill comes in wrong, then someone says they were supposed to call me back but “forgot”—and people say, oh, offices make mistakes, but I worked in offices for years, I supervised people, I know the difference between a mistake and a pattern.\n\nIt feels like information is being kept from me, like they’re trying to edge me out of decisions I’m supposed to be part of. And, you know, my sister doesn’t always notice these things the way I do—she’ll just say, “let it go,” but that’s how people get taken advantage of. So yes, I think someone is doing it on purpose, or at least letting it happen on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people are targeting you specifically?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Maybe it started with my sister’s stuff, her appointments being moved around and the bills being wrong, but I’m the one who catches it, so then I’m the problem to them. That’s how it feels.\n\nAnd I worked in offices, I know how messages are supposed to be handled. You don’t just “forget” over and over unless someone is keeping things from you or trying to make you look difficult. My sister says I should just let it go, but that’s easy to say when you’re not the one being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these events are somehow related to you or your life in a special way, beyond just being targeted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “special,” exactly… but it is related to me, because I’m the one who notices. I’m the one asking questions and keeping track. For months it’s been the same kind of thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back—and everyone acts like these are separate little mistakes.\n\nBut they’re not separate. When you’ve worked in offices like I have, you know how things are supposed to go. I used to supervise people doing this kind of work, and you learn very quickly when something is just sloppy and when information is being held back. And somehow it always ends with me being pushed out of decisions I should be part of. So yes, it feels connected to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people who are supposedly doing these things to you are plotting against you in a coordinated way?\n\nPatient: Coordinated… I don’t know if they’re all sitting in a room making a plan, if that’s what you mean. But it feels connected. For months now it’s the same pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved, then the bills are wrong, then someone “forgets” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the one left without the information.\n\nI worked in offices a long time, you know, and when you supervise people you learn how things are supposed to flow—messages, records, callbacks, all of that. My old manager used to say, “One mistake is a mistake, three mistakes is a system,” and that’s sort of what I keep thinking about. Everyone tells me it’s just errors, but I know when information is being kept from me. It’s like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I’m supposed to be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these events are happening because of something specific about you, such as your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s my thoughts, like I’m making it happen by thinking something. But my actions, maybe—because I keep asking questions. I keep noticing when things don’t add up.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, really. For months it’s been the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, bills come wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone says, oh, it’s just mistakes, but I worked in offices, I know how records and calls are supposed to be handled. If you keep pressing, some people don’t like that.\n\nIt reminds me of when I was supervising staff and someone would “misplace” paperwork after being corrected—anyway, that’s what I mean. It feels like because I’m the one keeping track, they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there could be another explanation for these events, other than someone intentionally trying to keep information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything is possible, sure. People do make mistakes. I’ve made mistakes in offices myself—once we had a whole stack of invoices filed under the wrong vendor because someone changed the label and nobody told the rest of us, and then everyone blamed the front desk.\n\nBut this is why I’m here, because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be mistaken about someone intentionally keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking… could I be mistaken. I mean, in a general sense, yes, people can be wrong. I know that. I supervised office staff for years, and half the job was sorting out misunderstandings—someone puts a note in the wrong folder, someone assumes someone else made the call, that kind of thing.\n\nBut this doesn’t feel like that. I’m here because for months now I keep seeing the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, the bills show up wrong, people “forget” to call me back, and then I’m the one who doesn’t have the information. And everyone keeps saying it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs about people keeping information from you could be considered unreasonable by others?\n\nPatient: I’m sure they would call it unreasonable. My sister already gives me that look, like I’m making too much of it. But I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern—appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in offices, so I notice these things. There’s a way information flows when people want you included, and there’s a way it suddenly gets messy when they don’t. So maybe other people think I’m overreacting, but to me it’s pretty clear they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you could change your mind about people keeping information from you if you found out more information?\n\nPatient: I mean… if there were real information, yes, I would look at it. I’m not someone who refuses paperwork or facts—honestly, in my old office, I was usually the one telling people to check the forms before jumping to conclusions. We had this one woman who would swear the copier was “eating” her reports, and it turned out she was saving them to the wrong folder every time.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern with my sister—appointments moved around, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the last to know. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after a while it stops looking like mistakes. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions. So… I guess if I found out more, maybe, but it would have to explain all of that, not just one little piece.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your belief about people keeping information from you is something that you are completely certain about, or is it something that you could potentially change your mind about?\n\nPatient: Completely certain is a strong phrase… I mean, I suppose anyone could change their mind if someone actually showed them something that made sense. I’m not trying to be stubborn for the sake of it.\n\nBut I’ve been around offices long enough to know when things are just normal confusion and when they keep happening in the same direction. With my sister, it’s been months of this—appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m the one left out, every time.\n\nSo could I change my mind? Maybe, if there were a real explanation. But right now, no, I feel pretty certain. It really seems like information is being kept from me, like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day thinking about the possibility that people are keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it. I don’t sit down with a timer or anything, but it comes up a lot during the day—especially when I’m waiting for a call back or a bill doesn’t look right, or my sister tells me something after the fact.\n\nI’ll try to go do something else, and then I’ll think, well, why didn’t they tell me that sooner? At work, you know, if one person was always left off the email chain, that usually meant something. So I can’t just brush it off.\n\nI’d say… several hours altogether, probably. Not all at once, but on and off. Because for months now it’s been the same pattern—appointments moved, bills wrong, people “forgetting” to call—and everyone says mistakes, but it feels too consistent. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people keeping information from you are doing it for a reason that doesn't make sense to most people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it “doesn’t make sense” to most people. I think if they saw the whole thing laid out, maybe it would make more sense. Like, in an office, if one person keeps getting left off meeting notes, and then their paperwork is always late, and then people say, “Oh, sorry, I forgot”—after a while you stop calling that random.\n\nWith my sister, it’s been the same kind of thing for months. Her appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people don’t call me back, and then everyone acts like I’m overreacting. But I know they’re keeping information from me. I think they’re trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nNow, whether other people understand the reason… I don’t know. People don’t always understand office politics either until they’re the one being cut out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on other things because you're thinking about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. I mean, I still get things done—I’m not sitting there unable to function—but it does pull my attention away.\n\nLike at work, if someone makes a routine mistake, something I probably would’ve just corrected before, I find myself pushing harder now. Asking why it happened, who told them what, whether anyone else knew. And then the conversation gets uncomfortable, because people look at me like I’m making more of it than I should. But in an office, details matter. I’ve supervised people long enough to know that.\n\nAnd with family meetings about my sister, it’s gotten tense. I’ll try to listen, but then I’m watching for what isn’t being said, or why I’m only hearing something at the last minute. So yes, it affects my focus some. I’m still managing my responsibilities, but people are definitely reacting differently to me now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble enjoying activities you used to like because of these thoughts about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. I still do things, I’m not just sitting around staring at the wall or anything. But it’s harder to enjoy them when part of my mind is going back over who said what, or why I heard something late.\n\nAnd it spills over. Family meetings have become tense, because I’m listening for what’s being left out, and at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I used to. I used to be able to let some things go, you know, depending on the situation.\n\nSo I’m still managing my responsibilities. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But it has started to affect how people respond to me, and that makes it harder to relax and enjoy things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yes… probably more than I should, if I’m being honest. It comes up a lot during the day because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister—appointments get moved, bills show up wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.

People do make mistakes. I’ve made mistakes in offices myself—once we had a whole stack of invoices filed under the wrong vendor because someone changed the label and nobody told the rest of us, and then everyone blamed the front desk.\n\nBut this is why I’m here, because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be mistaken about someone intentionally keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking… could I be mistaken. I mean, in a general sense, yes, people can be wrong. I know that. I supervised office staff for years, and half the job was sorting out misunderstandings—someone puts a note in the wrong folder, someone assumes someone else made the call, that kind of thing.\n\nBut this doesn’t feel like that. I’m here because for months now I keep seeing the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, the bills show up wrong, people “forget” to call me back, and then I’m the one who doesn’t have the information. And everyone keeps saying it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs about people keeping information from you could be considered unreasonable by others?\n\nPatient: I’m sure they would call it unreasonable. I’m not someone who refuses paperwork or facts—honestly, in my old office, I was usually the one telling people to check the forms before jumping to conclusions. My sister’s appointments get shifted, then a bill comes in wrong, then someone says they were supposed to call me back but “forgot”—and people say, oh, offices make mistakes, but I worked in offices for years, I supervised people, I know the difference between a mistake and a pattern.\n\nIt feels like information is being kept from me, like they’re trying to edge me out of decisions I’m supposed to be part of. And, you know, my sister doesn’t always notice these things the way I do—she’ll just say, “let it go,” but that’s how people get taken advantage of. So yes, I think someone is doing it on purpose, or at least letting it happen on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people are targeting you specifically?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Maybe it started with my sister’s stuff, her appointments being moved around and the bills being wrong, but I’m the one who catches it, so then I’m the problem to them. That’s how it feels.\n\nAnd I worked in offices, I know how messages are supposed to be handled. You don’t just “forget” over and over unless someone is keeping things from you or trying to make you look difficult. My sister says I should just let it go, but that’s easy to say when you’re not the one being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these events are somehow related to you or your life in a special way, beyond just being targeted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “special,” exactly… but it is related to me, because I’m the one who notices. I’m the one asking questions and keeping track. For months it’s been the same kind of thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back—and everyone acts like these are separate little mistakes.\n\nBut they’re not separate. When you’ve worked in offices like I have, you know how things are supposed to go. I used to supervise people doing this kind of work, and you learn very quickly when something is just sloppy and when information is being held back. And somehow it always ends with me being pushed out of decisions I should be part of. So yes, it feels connected to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people who are supposedly doing these things to you are plotting against you in a coordinated way?\n\nPatient: Coordinated… I don’t know if they’re all sitting in a room making a plan, if that’s what you mean. But it feels connected. For months now it’s the same pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved, then the bills are wrong, then someone “forgets” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the one left without the information.\n\nI worked in offices a long time, you know, and when you supervise people you learn how things are supposed to flow—messages, records, callbacks, all of that. My old manager used to say, “One mistake is a mistake, three mistakes is a system,” and that’s sort of what I keep thinking about. Everyone tells me it’s just errors, but I know when information is being kept from me. It’s like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I’m supposed to be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these events are happening because of something specific about you, such as your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s my thoughts, like I’m making it happen by thinking something. But my actions, maybe—because I keep asking questions. I keep noticing when things don’t add up.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, really. For months it’s been the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, bills come wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone says, oh, it’s just mistakes, but I worked in offices, I know how records and calls are supposed to be handled. If you keep pressing, some people don’t like that.\n\nIt reminds me of when I was supervising staff and someone would “misplace” paperwork after being corrected—anyway, that’s what I mean. It feels like because I’m the one keeping track, they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there could be another explanation for these events, other than someone intentionally trying to keep information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything is possible, sure. People do make mistakes. I’ve made mistakes in offices myself—once we had a whole stack of invoices filed under the wrong vendor because someone changed the label and nobody told the rest of us, and then everyone blamed the front desk.\n\nBut this is why I’m here, because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be mistaken about someone intentionally keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking… could I be mistaken. I mean, in a general sense, yes, people can be wrong. I know that. I supervised office staff for years, and half the job was sorting out misunderstandings—someone puts a note in the wrong folder, someone assumes someone else made the call, that kind of thing.\n\nBut this doesn’t feel like that. I’m here because for months now I keep seeing the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, the bills show up wrong, people “forget” to call me back, and then I’m the one who doesn’t have the information. And everyone keeps saying it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs about people keeping information from you could be considered unreasonable by others?\n\nPatient: I’m sure they would call it unreasonable. My sister already gives me that look, like I’m making too much of it. But I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern—appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in offices, so I notice these things. There’s a way information flows when people want you included, and there’s a way it suddenly gets messy when they don’t. So maybe other people think I’m overreacting, but to me it’s pretty clear they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you could change your mind about people keeping information from you if you found out more information?\n\nPatient: I mean… if there were real information, yes, I would look at it. I’m not someone who refuses paperwork or facts—honestly, in my old office, I was usually the one telling people to check the forms before jumping to conclusions. We had this one woman who would swear the copier was “eating” her reports, and it turned out she was saving them to the wrong folder every time.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern with my sister—appointments moved around, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the last to know. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after a while it stops looking like mistakes. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions. So… I guess if I found out more, maybe, but it would have to explain all of that, not just one little piece.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your belief about people keeping information from you is something that you are completely certain about, or is it something that you could potentially change your mind about?\n\nPatient: Completely certain is a strong phrase… I mean, I suppose anyone could change their mind if someone actually showed them something that made sense. I’m not trying to be stubborn for the sake of it.\n\nBut I’ve been around offices long enough to know when things are just normal confusion and when they keep happening in the same direction. With my sister, it’s been months of this—appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m the one left out, every time.\n\nSo could I change my mind? Maybe, if there were a real explanation. But right now, no, I feel pretty certain. It really seems like information is being kept from me, like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day thinking about the possibility that people are keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it. I don’t sit down with a timer or anything, but it comes up a lot during the day—especially when I’m waiting for a call back or a bill doesn’t look right, or my sister tells me something after the fact.\n\nI’ll try to go do something else, and then I’ll think, well, why didn’t they tell me that sooner? At work, you know, if one person was always left off the email chain, that usually meant something. So I can’t just brush it off.\n\nI’d say… several hours altogether, probably. Not all at once, but on and off. Because for months now it’s been the same pattern—appointments moved, bills wrong, people “forgetting” to call—and everyone says mistakes, but it feels too consistent. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people keeping information from you are doing it for a reason that doesn't make sense to most people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it “doesn’t make sense” to most people. I think if they saw the whole thing laid out, maybe it would make more sense. Like, in an office, if one person keeps getting left off meeting notes, and then their paperwork is always late, and then people say, “Oh, sorry, I forgot”—after a while you stop calling that random.\n\nWith my sister, it’s been the same kind of thing for months. Her appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people don’t call me back, and then everyone acts like I’m overreacting. But I know they’re keeping information from me. I think they’re trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nNow, whether other people understand the reason… I don’t know. People don’t always understand office politics either until they’re the one being cut out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on other things because you're thinking about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. I mean, I still get things done—I’m not sitting there unable to function—but it does pull my attention away.\n\nLike at work, if someone makes a routine mistake, something I probably would’ve just corrected before, I find myself pushing harder now. Asking why it happened, who told them what, whether anyone else knew. And then the conversation gets uncomfortable, because people look at me like I’m making more of it than I should. But in an office, details matter. I’ve supervised people long enough to know that.\n\nAnd with family meetings about my sister, it’s gotten tense. I’ll try to listen, but then I’m watching for what isn’t being said, or why I’m only hearing something at the last minute. So yes, it affects my focus some. I’m still managing my responsibilities, but people are definitely reacting differently to me now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble enjoying activities you used to like because of these thoughts about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. I still do things, I’m not just sitting around staring at the wall or anything. But it’s harder to enjoy them when part of my mind is going back over who said what, or why I heard something late.\n\nAnd it spills over. Family meetings have become tense, because I’m listening for what’s being left out, and at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I used to. I used to be able to let some things go, you know, depending on the situation.\n\nSo I’m still managing my responsibilities. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But it has started to affect how people respond to me, and that makes it harder to relax and enjoy things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about people keeping information from you more often than you think about other things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I should, if I’m being honest. It comes up a lot during the day because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister—appointments get moved, bills show up wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.\n\nAnd I know people will say, well, offices make mistakes, billing departments are a mess—believe me, I’ve worked around paperwork long enough to know that. I had one clerk years ago who could misfile a form three different ways before lunch. But this isn’t like that. This feels like information is being kept from me, like I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it a lot. Even when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of me is still putting the pieces together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know what evidence would change my mind, honestly. I mean, if this had happened once or twice, sure, I’d say maybe it was a mistake. Offices make mistakes, bills get entered wrong, people forget calls—I’ve supervised clerical work long enough to know that happens.

But this has been going on for months with my sister. Appointments get moved, bills show up wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back, and somehow I’m always finding out late. After a while it stops looking accidental. It feels like the same pattern over and over, like I’m being kept out of decisions.

Maybe if everyone sat down and showed me everything clearly, ahead of time, without things missing… maybe. But even then, I’d wonder why it took that much pushing to get it. So no, not easily. I think they are keeping information from me.

So yes, it feels connected to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people who are supposedly doing these things to you are plotting against you in a coordinated way?\n\nPatient: Coordinated… I don’t know if they’re all sitting in a room making a plan, if that’s what you mean. But it feels connected. For months now it’s the same pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved, then the bills are wrong, then someone “forgets” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the one left without the information.\n\nI worked in offices a long time, you know, and when you supervise people you learn how things are supposed to flow—messages, records, callbacks, all of that. My old manager used to say, “One mistake is a mistake, three mistakes is a system,” and that’s sort of what I keep thinking about. Everyone tells me it’s just errors, but I know when information is being kept from me. It’s like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I’m supposed to be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these events are happening because of something specific about you, such as your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s my thoughts, like I’m making it happen by thinking something. But my actions, maybe—because I keep asking questions. I keep noticing when things don’t add up.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, really. For months it’s been the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, bills come wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone says, oh, it’s just mistakes, but I worked in offices, I know how records and calls are supposed to be handled. If you keep pressing, some people don’t like that.\n\nIt reminds me of when I was supervising staff and someone would “misplace” paperwork after being corrected—anyway, that’s what I mean. It feels like because I’m the one keeping track, they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there could be another explanation for these events, other than someone intentionally trying to keep information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything is possible, sure. People do make mistakes. I’ve made mistakes in offices myself—once we had a whole stack of invoices filed under the wrong vendor because someone changed the label and nobody told the rest of us, and then everyone blamed the front desk.\n\nBut this is why I’m here, because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be mistaken about someone intentionally keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking… could I be mistaken. I mean, in a general sense, yes, people can be wrong. I know that. I supervised office staff for years, and half the job was sorting out misunderstandings—someone puts a note in the wrong folder, someone assumes someone else made the call, that kind of thing.\n\nBut this doesn’t feel like that. I’m here because for months now I keep seeing the same pattern: my sister’s appointments get moved, the bills show up wrong, people “forget” to call me back, and then I’m the one who doesn’t have the information. And everyone keeps saying it’s just mistakes, but I know what it looks like when information is being kept from someone. It feels like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs about people keeping information from you could be considered unreasonable by others?\n\nPatient: I’m sure they would call it unreasonable. My sister already gives me that look, like I’m making too much of it. But I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern—appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in offices, so I notice these things. There’s a way information flows when people want you included, and there’s a way it suddenly gets messy when they don’t. So maybe other people think I’m overreacting, but to me it’s pretty clear they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you could change your mind about people keeping information from you if you found out more information?\n\nPatient: I mean… if there were real information, yes, I would look at it. I’m not someone who refuses paperwork or facts—honestly, in my old office, I was usually the one telling people to check the forms before jumping to conclusions. We had this one woman who would swear the copier was “eating” her reports, and it turned out she was saving them to the wrong folder every time.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the same pattern with my sister—appointments moved around, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m always the last to know. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after a while it stops looking like mistakes. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions. So… I guess if I found out more, maybe, but it would have to explain all of that, not just one little piece.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your belief about people keeping information from you is something that you are completely certain about, or is it something that you could potentially change your mind about?\n\nPatient: Completely certain is a strong phrase… I mean, I suppose anyone could change their mind if someone actually showed them something that made sense. I’m not trying to be stubborn for the sake of it.\n\nBut I’ve been around offices long enough to know when things are just normal confusion and when they keep happening in the same direction. With my sister, it’s been months of this—appointments getting moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they “forgot” to call me back. And somehow I’m the one left out, every time.\n\nSo could I change my mind? Maybe, if there were a real explanation. But right now, no, I feel pretty certain. It really seems like information is being kept from me, like they’re trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day thinking about the possibility that people are keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it. I don’t sit down with a timer or anything, but it comes up a lot during the day—especially when I’m waiting for a call back or a bill doesn’t look right, or my sister tells me something after the fact.\n\nI’ll try to go do something else, and then I’ll think, well, why didn’t they tell me that sooner? At work, you know, if one person was always left off the email chain, that usually meant something. So I can’t just brush it off.\n\nI’d say… several hours altogether, probably. Not all at once, but on and off. Because for months now it’s been the same pattern—appointments moved, bills wrong, people “forgetting” to call—and everyone says mistakes, but it feels too consistent. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people keeping information from you are doing it for a reason that doesn't make sense to most people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it “doesn’t make sense” to most people. I think if they saw the whole thing laid out, maybe it would make more sense. Like, in an office, if one person keeps getting left off meeting notes, and then their paperwork is always late, and then people say, “Oh, sorry, I forgot”—after a while you stop calling that random.\n\nWith my sister, it’s been the same kind of thing for months. Her appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, people don’t call me back, and then everyone acts like I’m overreacting. But I know they’re keeping information from me. I think they’re trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nNow, whether other people understand the reason… I don’t know. People don’t always understand office politics either until they’re the one being cut out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on other things because you're thinking about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. I mean, I still get things done—I’m not sitting there unable to function—but it does pull my attention away.\n\nLike at work, if someone makes a routine mistake, something I probably would’ve just corrected before, I find myself pushing harder now. Asking why it happened, who told them what, whether anyone else knew. And then the conversation gets uncomfortable, because people look at me like I’m making more of it than I should. But in an office, details matter. I’ve supervised people long enough to know that.\n\nAnd with family meetings about my sister, it’s gotten tense. I’ll try to listen, but then I’m watching for what isn’t being said, or why I’m only hearing something at the last minute. So yes, it affects my focus some. I’m still managing my responsibilities, but people are definitely reacting differently to me now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble enjoying activities you used to like because of these thoughts about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. I still do things, I’m not just sitting around staring at the wall or anything. But it’s harder to enjoy them when part of my mind is going back over who said what, or why I heard something late.\n\nAnd it spills over. Family meetings have become tense, because I’m listening for what’s being left out, and at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I used to. I used to be able to let some things go, you know, depending on the situation.\n\nSo I’m still managing my responsibilities. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But it has started to affect how people respond to me, and that makes it harder to relax and enjoy things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about people keeping information from you more often than you think about other things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I should, if I’m being honest. It comes up a lot during the day because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister—appointments get moved, bills show up wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.\n\nAnd I know people will say, well, offices make mistakes, billing departments are a mess—believe me, I’ve worked around paperwork long enough to know that. I had one clerk years ago who could misfile a form three different ways before lunch. But this isn’t like that. This feels like information is being kept from me, like I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it a lot. Even when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of me is still putting the pieces together.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what evidence would change my mind, honestly. I mean, if this had happened once or twice, sure, I’d say maybe it was a mistake. Offices make mistakes, bills get entered wrong, people forget calls—I’ve supervised clerical work long enough to know that happens.\n\nBut this has been going on for months with my sister. Appointments get moved, bills show up wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back, and somehow I’m always finding out late. After a while it stops looking accidental. It feels like the same pattern over and over, like I’m being kept out of decisions.\n\nMaybe if everyone sat down and showed me everything clearly, ahead of time, without things missing… maybe. But even then, I’d wonder why it took that much pushing to get it. So no, not easily. I think they are keeping information from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Family meetings have become tense, because I’m listening for what’s being left out, and at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I used to. I used to be able to let some things go, you know, depending on the situation.\n\nSo I’m still managing my responsibilities. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But it has started to affect how people respond to me, and that makes it harder to relax and enjoy things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about people keeping information from you more often than you think about other things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I should, if I’m being honest. It comes up a lot during the day because it’s not just one thing. For months now it’s been the same pattern with my sister—appointments get moved, bills show up wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back.\n\nAnd I know people will say, well, offices make mistakes, billing departments are a mess—believe me, I’ve worked around paperwork long enough to know that. I had one clerk years ago who could misfile a form three different ways before lunch. But this isn’t like that. This feels like information is being kept from me, like I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it a lot. Even when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of me is still putting the pieces together.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what evidence would change my mind, honestly. I mean, if this had happened once or twice, sure, I’d say maybe it was a mistake. Offices make mistakes, bills get entered wrong, people forget calls—I’ve supervised clerical work long enough to know that happens.\n\nBut this has been going on for months with my sister. Appointments get moved, bills show up wrong, people say they “forgot” to call me back, and somehow I’m always finding out late. After a while it stops looking accidental. It feels like the same pattern over and over, like I’m being kept out of decisions.\n\nMaybe if everyone sat down and showed me everything clearly, ahead of time, without things missing… maybe. But even then, I’d wonder why it took that much pushing to get it. So no, not easily. I think they are keeping information from me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your thoughts about people keeping information from you caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it has. Mostly with family… those meetings get tense now. I’m listening so closely for what’s not being said that I know I probably come across sharp, but if people would just be straightforward, it wouldn’t turn into that.

And, I mean, it reminds me of work too—because in an office, when one small thing gets entered wrong, it can turn into three different problems by Friday. I’ve seen that for years. But lately I’ve been pressing people harder over routine mistakes than I used to, and a few conversations have gotten uncomfortable because of it.

I’m still doing what I need to do. I don’t want anyone thinking I’ve fallen apart. But yes, it’s affected how people respond to me, and that’s made things strained.
